Lemma 4.8

The Hecke eigensystem \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\Lambda }:\mathbb {T}^S \rightarrow \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}Λ~:TS→O attached to the contragredient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\pi }$$\end{document}π~ is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \circ \theta $$\end{document}Λ∘θ.

Proof

It is enough to show the analogous fact after base changing from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C and working at a single place. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\notin S$$\end{document}v∉S be a finite place, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$G=\textrm{GL}_n(F_v)$$\end{document}G=GLn(Fv), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K=\textrm{GL}_n(\mathcal {O}_{F_v})$$\end{document}K=GLn(OFv), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$V=\pi _v$$\end{document}V=πv.

It is well known that the restriction map induces an isomorphism13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\widetilde{V})^K \xrightarrow {\sim } \widetilde{V^K} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(V~)K→∼VK~so in particular \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\widetilde{V})^K$$\end{document}(V~)K is 1-dimensional.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in G$$\end{document}g∈G. By the same proof as [20, Lemma 5.5.1 (c)], there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_1,\dots ,g_m\in G$$\end{document}g1,⋯,gm∈G such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K g K= \sqcup _i g_i K = \sqcup K g_i$$\end{document}KgK=⊔igiK=⊔Kgi. For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f\in (\widetilde{V})^K$$\end{document}f∈(V~)K and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in V^K$$\end{document}v∈VK,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} ([KgK]f)(v)=(\sum g_i\cdot f)(v)=f(\sum g_i^{-1}v) = f([Kg^{-1}K]v)=\lambda ([Kg^{-1}K])f(v). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}([KgK]f)(v)=(∑gi·f)(v)=f(∑gi-1v)=f([Kg-1K]v)=λ([Kg-1K])f(v).By (13), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[KgK]f=\lambda ([Kg^{-1}K])f$$\end{document}[KgK]f=λ([Kg-1K])f, as desired. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Proposition 4.9

(Poincaré duality) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$d=dim X_U$$\end{document}d=dimXU. The cup product induces a perfect pairing\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:H^i_c(X_U,M_\mu )/(\mathcal {O}-\text {tors}) \times H^{d-i}(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )/(\mathcal {O}-\text {tors}) \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:Hci(XU,Mμ)/(O-tors)×Hd-i(XU,Mμ∨)/(O-tors)→Owhere20\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_\mu ^\vee ={{\,\textrm{Hom}\,}}_\mathcal {O}(M_\mu ,\mathcal {O}).$$\end{document}Mμ∨=HomO(Mμ,O). If S is a finite set of finite places such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$U_v=\textrm{GL}_n(\mathcal {O}_v)$$\end{document}Uv=GLn(Ov) for all finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\notin S$$\end{document}v∉S, then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}tx,y] = [x, \theta (t)y] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[tx,y]=[x,θ(t)y]for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathcal {H}^S(G^S,U^S)$$\end{document}t∈HS(GS,US), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in H^i_c(X_U,M_\mu )/(\mathcal {O}-\text {tors})$$\end{document}x∈Hci(XU,Mμ)/(O-tors), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y\in H^{d-i}(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )/(\mathcal {O}-\text {tors})$$\end{document}y∈Hd-i(XU,Mμ∨)/(O-tors).

A version of this is proved in [21, Theorem 4.8.9], but the proof is not that easy. We shall deduce this from Verdier duality instead.

Proof

As stated in [7, Proposition 2.2.20], we have by Verdier duality an isomorphism\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} RHom_\mathcal {O}(R\Gamma _c(X_U,M_\mu ),\mathcal {O}) \cong R\Gamma (X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )[d] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RHomO(RΓc(XU,Mμ),O)≅RΓ(XU,Mμ∨)[d]in the derived category of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-modules \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D(\mathcal {O})$$\end{document}D(O). For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Corollary 4.10

Assume \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-torsion free and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p>2$$\end{document}p>2. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}\times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}} \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~→O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ] \times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}[\tilde{\epsilon }] \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ]×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~[ϵ~]→O are both perfect pairings.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ induces21 an isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})\cong \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^t_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}).$$\end{document}TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m)≅TS(H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~).

Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}\times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}} \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~→O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ] \times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}[\tilde{\epsilon }] \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ]×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~[ϵ~]→O are both perfect pairings.

Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}\times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}} \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~→O and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ] \times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}[\tilde{\epsilon }] \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ]×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~[ϵ~]→O are both perfect pairings.

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ induces21 an isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})\cong \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^t_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}).$$\end{document}TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m)≅TS(H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~).

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ induces21 an isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})\cong \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^t_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}).$$\end{document}TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m)≅TS(H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~).

Proof

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_1:\mathbb {T}^S \rightarrow \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu ))$$\end{document}q1:TS→TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q_2:\mathbb {T}^S \rightarrow \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee ))$$\end{document}q2:TS→TS(H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)) be the quotient maps. Note that the image of these two maps are finite \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-algebras, so they are product of local rings.

Claim

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_i \subset \mathbb {T}^S$$\end{document}mi⊂TS be maximal ideals containing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ker q_i$$\end{document}kerqi. Then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_{\mathfrak {m}_1}, \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\mathfrak {m}_2}]=0$$\end{document}[H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m1,H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m2]=0 unless \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_2=\theta (\mathfrak {m}_1)$$\end{document}m2=θ(m1).

To show this, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A:= H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})_{\mathfrak {m}_1}, B:= H^{t}_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}}^\vee )_{\mathfrak {m}_2}$$\end{document}A:=H!b(XU,Mμ,C)m1,B:=H!t(XU,Mμ,C∨)m2. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[A,B]\ne 0$$\end{document}[A,B]≠0. By [19, section 2.5.1],14\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A=H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})[\mathfrak {m}_1^\infty ]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A=H!b(XU,Mμ,C)[m1∞].By Lemma 2.16, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S_\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}TCS acts semisimply on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}}).$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,C). As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S$$\end{document}TS is commutative, every simple \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S_\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}TCS-submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,C) is 1 dimensional. It follows that with respect to a suitable basis of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,C), every element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S_\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}TCS acts on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,C) by a diagonal matrix. Hence by (14), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A=H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})[\mathfrak {m}_1]$$\end{document}A=H!b(XU,Mμ,C)[m1]. Similarly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B = H^{t}_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}}^\vee )[\mathfrak {m}_2].$$\end{document}B=H!t(XU,Mμ,C∨)[m2].

By assumption, there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$a\in A$$\end{document}a∈A such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[a,B]\ne 0$$\end{document}[a,B]≠0. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\in \mathfrak {m}_1$$\end{document}t∈m1. For all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in B$$\end{document}b∈B,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} 0=[ta,b]=[a,\theta (t)b], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}0=[ta,b]=[a,θ(t)b],so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (t)$$\end{document}θ(t) is not surjective as an endomorphism on B and hence not injective, so there exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c\in B-\{0\}$$\end{document}c∈B-{0} such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (t)c=0$$\end{document}θ(t)c=0. As \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_2$$\end{document}m2 is a maximal ideal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S$$\end{document}TS, the annihilators of c in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S$$\end{document}TS is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}_2$$\end{document}m2, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (t)\in \mathfrak {m}_2$$\end{document}θ(t)∈m2. This means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\mathfrak {m}_1)\subset \mathfrak {m}_2$$\end{document}θ(m1)⊂m2, so \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (\mathfrak {m}_1)=\mathfrak {m}_2$$\end{document}θ(m1)=m2. This proves the claim.

We know that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu ) = \bigoplus _{\begin{array}{c} \mathfrak {m}_1\lhd \mathbb {T}^S \\ \mathfrak {m}_1\supset \ker q_1 \end{array}}\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_{\mathfrak {m}_1} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H¯!b(XU,Mμ)=⨁m1⊲TSm1⊃kerq1H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m1and similarly for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )$$\end{document}H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨). Using this and the claim, we can deduce the first part of (a) by the same argument as [1, section 4.2.4] under our assumption that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-torsion free. Let us just remark that, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m appears because we have a long exact sequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dots \rightarrow H^i_c(X_U,M_\mu )\rightarrow H^i(X_U,M_\mu ) \rightarrow H^i(\partial X_U,M_\mu )\rightarrow \dots $$\end{document}⋯→Hci(XU,Mμ)→Hi(XU,Mμ)→Hi(∂XU,Mμ)→⋯

For the second part of (a), note that we have a decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}= \bigoplus _{\epsilon _1\in \widehat{K_\infty /K_\infty ^\circ }}\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_{\mathfrak {m}}[\epsilon _1] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m=⨁ϵ1∈K∞/K∞∘^H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ1]since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p>2$$\end{document}p>2. The perfectness then follows from the first part and the proof of [1, Proposition 3.3.1]. (The proof there works here in view of the decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{i}(\mathfrak g, K_\infty ^0,\pi _\infty \otimes _\mathbb {C}M_{\mu ,\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}Hi(g,K∞0,π∞⊗CMμ,C) in the last part of the proof of 2.12.)

Part (b) follows from part (a) and the same argument as [7, Corollary 2.2.21], namely the commutativity of the diagram \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Lemma 4.11

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ,\tilde{\phi }$$\end{document}ϕ,ϕ~ be cusp forms in the space of cusp forms affording \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi ,\tilde{\pi }$$\end{document}π,π~ respectively. Define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \phi ,\tilde{\phi } \rangle = \int _{A_G G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)} \phi (g)\tilde{\phi }(g) dg. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕ,ϕ~⟩=∫AGG(F)\G(AF)ϕ(g)ϕ~(g)dg.Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \phi ,\tilde{\phi } \rangle = \frac{\prod _{v\mid \infty }\mathfrak c_v^\sharp (w_v,\tilde{w}_v) \cdot L(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^\circ )}{\alpha _F\; \mathfrak p_{ram}(\pi )}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕ,ϕ~⟩=∏v∣∞cv♯(wv,w~v)·L(1,π,Ad∘)αFpram(π),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _F:=\frac{\hat{\Phi }_f(0)}{n {{\,\textrm{Res}\,}}_{s=1}\tilde{\zeta }_F(s)}$$\end{document}αF:=Φ^f(0)nRess=1ζ~F(s) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _f$$\end{document}Φf the characteristic function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\prod _{v\not \mid \infty }\mathcal {O}_{F_v}^n$$\end{document}∏v∤∞OFvn, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\Phi }_f$$\end{document}Φ^f its Fourier transform, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\zeta }_F$$\end{document}ζ~F the completed zeta function. The measures, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak c_v^\sharp (w_v,\tilde{w}_v)$$\end{document}cv♯(wv,w~v), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak p_{ram}(\pi )$$\end{document}pram(π) are defined as in [1, section 2]. Also, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^\circ )$$\end{document}L(1,π,Ad∘) is the value at 1 of the Langlands L-function (see e.g. [15, section 12.7]).

Proof

Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\int _{A_G G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)} = \int _{Z(\mathbb {A}_F) G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)} \int _{A_G G(F)\backslash Z(\mathbb {A}_F)G(F)}$$\end{document}∫AGG(F)\G(AF)=∫Z(AF)G(F)\G(AF)∫AGG(F)\Z(AF)G(F), where Z is the centre of G. Also, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$A_G G(F)\backslash Z(\mathbb {A}_F)G(F) = F^\times \backslash \mathbb {A}^1_F$$\end{document}AGG(F)\Z(AF)G(F)=F×\AF1, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {A}^1_F:=\{x\in \mathbb {A}_F: |x|_{\mathbb {A}_F}=1\}.$$\end{document}AF1:={x∈AF:|x|AF=1}. It follows that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \langle \phi ,\tilde{\phi } \rangle = vol(F^\times \backslash \mathbb {A}_F^1)\int _{Z(\mathbb {A}_F)G(F)\backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)} \phi (g)\tilde{\phi }(g) dg. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕ,ϕ~⟩=vol(F×\AF1)∫Z(AF)G(F)\G(AF)ϕ(g)ϕ~(g)dg.The result now follows from [1, equation (2.2.11)]. They obtained their result by relating the Petersson inner product with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0)$$\end{document}L(1,π,Ad0) by the Rankin-Selberg method and using the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L(s,\pi \times \tilde{\pi })=\tilde{\zeta }_F(s)L(s,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0).$$\end{document}L(s,π×π~)=ζ~F(s)L(s,π,Ad0). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

,b,\epsilon }, \mathfrak p_{\tilde{\pi },t,\tilde{\epsilon }}$$\end{document}pπ,b,ϵ,pπ~,t,ϵ~ are defined in Definition 4.7.22

Proof

This is more or less what [1, section 3.3.3] obtained, except that our space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_U$$\end{document}XU is different from the locally symmetric spaces they used. As in [1, p.658], we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\mathfrak p_{\pi ,b,\epsilon }\vartheta ^\circ _{b,\epsilon },\mathfrak p_{\tilde{\pi },t,\tilde{\epsilon }}\tilde{\vartheta }^\circ _{t,\tilde{\epsilon }}]&= \frac{1}{vol(U)} \int _{G(F) \backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)/K_\infty ^\circ U} \varsigma \\&= \frac{1}{vol(U)}\int _{G(F) \backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)/A_G U} \varsigma \\&= \int _{G(F) \backslash G(\mathbb {A}_F)/A_G} \varsigma \\&= \frac{L(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0)}{\alpha _F \,\mathfrak p_{ram}(\pi )\mathfrak p_\infty (\pi )} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[pπ,b,ϵϑb,ϵ∘,pπ~,t,ϵ~ϑ~t,ϵ~∘]=1vol(U)∫G(F)\G(AF)/K∞∘Uς=1vol(U)∫G(F)\G(AF)/AGUς=∫G(F)\G(AF)/AGς=L(1,π,Ad0)αFpram(π)p∞(π)where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varsigma $$\end{document}ς has the same meaning as that in [1, p.658] and in the last equality we used Lemma 4.11 instead of [1, equation (2.2.11)]. Dividing both sides by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak p_{\pi ,b,\epsilon }\mathfrak p_{\tilde{\pi },t,\tilde{\epsilon }}$$\end{document}pπ,b,ϵpπ~,t,ϵ~ gives the result. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.13

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha _F$$\end{document}αF depends only on F, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak p_{ram}(\pi )$$\end{document}pram(π) depends only on the ramified components of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak p_{\infty }(\pi )$$\end{document}p∞(π) depends only on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\infty $$\end{document}π∞.

We can now prove our first main theorem.

Theorem 4.14

Let the notation be as at the start of Section 4. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-torsion free. Then\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta _{\pi ,b,\epsilon }=\eta _{\tilde{\pi },t,\tilde{\epsilon }}=(L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ηπ,b,ϵ=ηπ~,t,ϵ~=(Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ)).

Proof

Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-torsion free, the cup product gives a perfect pairing\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}\,,\,]:\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ] \times \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}[\tilde{\epsilon }] \rightarrow \mathcal {O}\end{aligned}$$\end{document}[,]:H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ]×H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~[ϵ~]→Oby Corollary 4.10.23 We would like to apply Lemma 3.5. The conditions in part (b) of that lemma is satisfied by Lemma 4.5 and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\pi ^\infty )^U$$\end{document}(π∞)U and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tilde{\pi }^\infty )^U$$\end{document}(π~∞)U are one dimensional.

Recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda :T\rightarrow \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}λ:T→O is the Hecke eigensystem for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π. This factors through \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})$$\end{document}TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m) because the action of the Hecke algebra on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{H}^*_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}H¯!∗(XU,Mμ)m preserves degree and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π is isomorphic to a submodule of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}\otimes _\mathcal {O}\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m⊗OC.

Similar to how we defined the idempotent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e\in T_K$$\end{document}e∈TK using \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda :T\rightarrow \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}λ:T→O, we can define an idempotent \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e'\in \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})_K$$\end{document}e′∈TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m)K using the induced map on the quotient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})\rightarrow \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m)→O. It is clear from the definitions of e and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e'$$\end{document}e′ that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e'$$\end{document}e′ is the image of e under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}\twoheadrightarrow \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m})$$\end{document}T↠TS(H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m).

The same argument (with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ)m replaced by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^t_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}$$\end{document}H!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~) works for the contragredient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\pi }$$\end{document}π~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\lambda }$$\end{document}λ~, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{e}, \tilde{e}'$$\end{document}e~,e~′ and we know \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{e}'$$\end{document}e~′ is the image of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{e}$$\end{document}e~ under \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{T} \twoheadrightarrow \mathbb {T}^S(\overline{H}^t_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}})$$\end{document}T~↠TS(H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~).

By Lemma 4.8, we know \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{\lambda }=\lambda \circ \theta $$\end{document}λ~=λ∘θ. It follows from definition and part (b) of Corollary 4.10 that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (e')=\theta (\tilde{e}')$$\end{document}θ(e′)=θ(e~′). Thus, for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\in \overline{H}^b_!(X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ]$$\end{document}x∈H¯!b(XU,Mμ)m[ϵ], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y \in \overline{H}^{t}_!(X_U,M_\mu ^\vee )_{\tilde{\mathfrak {m}}}[\tilde{\epsilon }]$$\end{document}y∈H¯!t(XU,Mμ∨)m~[ϵ~], we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[}ex,y]=[e'x,y]=[x,\theta (e')y]=[x,\tilde{e}' y]=[x,\tilde{e} y]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[ex,y]=[e′x,y]=[x,θ(e′)y]=[x,e~′y]=[x,e~y].From this24 and the fact that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e,e'$$\end{document}e,e′ are idempotents, we deduce that all the conditions of Lemma 3.5 are satisfied.

By Lemma 3.5 and Lemma 4.12,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta _{\pi ,b,\epsilon }=\eta _{\tilde{\pi },t,\tilde{\epsilon }}= [\vartheta ^\circ _{b,\epsilon },\tilde{\vartheta }^\circ _{t,\tilde{\epsilon }}] =(L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ηπ,b,ϵ=ηπ~,t,ϵ~=[ϑb,ϵ∘,ϑ~t,ϵ~∘]=(Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ)).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 4.15

The theorem is a generalisation of [4, Proposition 4.12 first part and Lemma 5.6(iv)], where analogous results were obtained for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GL}_2$$\end{document}GL2 over a totally real field and over an imaginary quadratic field.25

An analogous result is stated in [1, section 4]. It differs from our result in the following ways: the result there is for the product of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )$$\end{document}Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ) over all permissible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ while our result is for each individual permissible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon $$\end{document}ϵ. Additionally, we localize at a maximal ideal throughout, which is necessary for relating congruence ideals to Selmer groups (see Theorem 4.26 below). Localisation also makes some results slightly harder to prove, but requires a weaker hypothesis that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ)m, rather than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b(\partial X_U,M_\mu )$$\end{document}Hb(∂XU,Mμ), is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}$$\end{document}O-torsion free (see Lemma 4.19 below for a case where this holds). Although this weaker hypothesis was mentioned in [1, page 669], the necessary (small) adjustments to their proof were not worked out. We have provided more detailed arguments for certain parts. Furthermore, to facilitate the use of some results in [7] and [14], we work with a different locally symmetric space, preventing us from applying some results from [1] directly. For the same reason, our \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )$$\end{document}Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ) is slightly different from theirs.

Remark 4.16

It may be possible to determine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak p_\infty (\pi )$$\end{document}p∞(π) explicitly as a power of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi i$$\end{document}2πi using techniques of [22], but we have not attempted this. In that paper, they precisely determined some archimedean zeta-integrals by replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π with simpler automorphic representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi '$$\end{document}π′ with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi _\infty \cong \pi _\infty '$$\end{document}π∞≅π∞′. Here, ’simpler’ means \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi '$$\end{document}π′ is automorphically induced from a Hecke character or is an isobaric sum of Hecke characters. This approach allows them to relate the L-function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π to those of Hecke characters, which, in turn, are related to CM periods by results of Blasius.

Now, we will explain why this is related to congruences of automorphic representations. Roughly speaking, if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )$$\end{document}Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ) is not a p-adic unit, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π is congruent to another automorphic representation. The converse holds if the maximal ideal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m is non-Eisenstein.

Proof

Abusing notation, we shall identify \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {Q}}_p$$\end{document}Q¯p with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {C}$$\end{document}C using our fixed isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota $$\end{document}ι. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi '\cong \pi $$\end{document}π′≅π iff \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda '=\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ′=Λ by the strong multiplicity one theorem (where we regard \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ as having codomain in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\mathbb {Q}}_p$$\end{document}Q¯p).

Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varpi \mid L^{alg}(1,\pi ,{{\,\textrm{Ad}\,}}^0,\epsilon )$$\end{document}ϖ∣Lalg(1,π,Ad0,ϵ). By Theorem 4.14 and Lemma 3.4, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _\pi \ne \mathcal {O}$$\end{document}ηπ≠O. Suppose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathcal {O}})_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ]/\mathcal {O}\text {-torsion}$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,O)m[ϵ]/O-torsion is a free T-module, say, of rank d, and there is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi '$$\end{document}π′ satisfying the statement of the corollary. Remark 4.18

A version of Corollary 4.17 appears in [1, Theorem 4.3.1], but the conditional converse is not stated explicitly and is not proved. An analogue of Corollary 4.17 in the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GL}_2$$\end{document}GL2 is also proved in [23, Theorem 5.25] under certain conditions for minimal and ordinary eigenforms.

We think the condition for the converse, namely that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^b_!(X_U,M_{\mu ,\mathcal {O}})_\mathfrak {m}[\epsilon ]/\mathcal {O}\text {-torsion}$$\end{document}H!b(XU,Mμ,O)m[ϵ]/O-torsion is a free T-module, is not unreasonable. When F is a CM field, it should be possible to verify this using the Taylor-Wiles method if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m is non-Eisenstein and the Galois representation attached to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\pi $$\end{document}π is a minimally ramified deformation of its residual representation. However, under our current knowledge of Galois representations, such an approach will require a lot of extra assumptions and conjectures (such as the vanishing of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^i(X_U,k)_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}Hi(XU,k)m outside the cuspidal range, existence of Hecke algebra valued Galois representations (without nilpotent ideals), local-global compatibility of such representations). Therefore, we do not pursue this approach here. See, however, [24] for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{GL}_2$$\end{document}GL2 case.27 It may also be possible to extend the freeness to the non-minimal case using the methods of [25, 26].

Now, assume in addition that F is a CM field that contains an imaginary quadratic field and S comes via pullback from a set of finite places of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}Q which contains p and all places at which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F/\mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}F/Q is ramified. These conditions guarantee the existence of various Galois representations [27, section V.4]. We say that a maximal ideal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(H^*(X_U,M_{\mu }))$$\end{document}TS(H∗(XU,Mμ)) is non-Eisenstein if the residual Galois representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\overline{\rho }_\mathfrak {m}:{{\,\textrm{Gal}\,}}(\overline{F}/F)\rightarrow \textrm{GL}_n(\overline{ \mathbb {F}}_p)$$\end{document}ρ¯m:Gal(F¯/F)→GLn(F¯p) attached to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m is absolutely irreducible.

Lemma 4.19

Let the notation be as above. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}m be a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^S(H^*(X_U,M_{\mu }))$$\end{document}TS(H∗(XU,Mμ)). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*(\partial X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}=0$$\end{document}H∗(∂XU,Mμ)m=0,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*(\partial X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}=0$$\end{document}H∗(∂XU,Mμ)m=0,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*(X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}=H^*_!(X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}H∗(XU,Mμ)m=H!∗(XU,Mμ)m,

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^*(X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}=H^*_!(X_U,M_{\mu })_\mathfrak {m}$$\end{document}H∗(XU,Mμ)m=H!∗(XU,Mμ)m,
